Equational Reasoning Modulo Commutativity in Languages with Binders
Ali K. Caires-Santos, Maribel Fernández, Daniele Nantes-Sobrinho

Abstract
Many formal languages include binders as well as operators that satisfy equational axioms, such as commutativity. Here we consider the nominal language, a general formal framework which provides support for the representation of binders, freshness conditions and α \alpha -renaming. Rather than relying on the usual freshness constraints, we introduce a nominal algebra which employs permutation fixed-point constraints in α \alpha -equivalence judgements, seamlessly integrating commutativity into the reasoning process. We establish its proof-theoretical properties and provide a sound and complete semantics in the setting of nominal sets. Additionally, we propose a novel algorithm for nominal unification modulo commutativity, which we prove terminating and correct. By leveraging fixed-point constraints, our approach ensures a finitary unification theory, unlike standard methods relying on freshness constraints. This framework offers a robust foundation for structural induction and recursion over syntax with binders and commutative operators, enabling reasoning in settings such as first-order logic and the π \pi -calculus.



1
Introduction

In computer science and logic, formal languages abstract away concrete syntax details, focusing on abstract syntax to capture structural properties. However, conventional abstract syntax falls short for languages with variable-binding constructs.
The formalisation of languages with binding is challenging and requires specialised tools: one of the aims of the POPLMark challenge
[
7
]
was to explore and compare methods for the formalisation of binding operators. Many different techniques were proposed to address the challenge, for example, the approach based on higher-order abstract syntax (HOAS) is described in the survey
[
14
]
by Felty et al.
Here we follow the nominal approach, introduced by Gabbay and Pitts
[
19
]
: Binders are specified using an abstraction construct of the form
[a]t
, where
a
is a name and
t
an arbitrary expression (read “abstract
a
in
t
”).

Nominal syntax
[
19
]
makes a distinction between
names

a,b,...
(also called atoms), which can be abstracted but cannot be substituted, and
(meta-)variables

X,Y,...
(also called
unkowns
), which can be substituted but not abstracted.
The
\alpha
-equivalence relation
\approx
is defined using the notion of support, or equivalently, the notion of freshness, a concept that generalises the idea of an atom not being free in a term:
a
is
fresh
for
t
, written
a\#t
, if
a
is not in the support of
t
.

More generally, in languages where operators are subject to equational axioms
E
, such as associativity (
\mathtt{A}
) and commutativity (
\mathtt{C}
), the equivalence relation of interest is generated by
\alpha
and
E
.
A well-known example is first-order logic with
\forall
and
\exists
quantifiers and conjunction and disjunction connectives; similarly, in Milner’s
\pi
-calculus expressions are considered modulo a structural congruence.
Effective reasoning techniques for such languages are essential, particularly when proving properties about them in a proof assistant. A common subproblem in this context is solving unification problems—finding the appropriate
substitution
that equates two expressions—when applying lemmas in automated proofs.

Although unification of first-order terms modulo commutativity is decidable and finitary, and nominal unification is unitary (every solvable problem has a unique most general unifier), unification modulo
\alpha
and
\mathtt{C}
is not finitary if solutions are expressed using substitutions and freshness contexts
[
4
]
. This motivated the study of a version of nominal syntax where the
\alpha
-equivalence relation is specified using permutation fixed-point constraints
\pi\curlywedge t
(read “
\pi
fixes
t
)” instead of freshness constraints
[
5
]
.
However, the derivation relation defined in
[
5
]
is sound for strong nominal algebras
[
21
,
20
]
only (that is, the carriers should be strong
1
1

1

A nominal set is strong iff its elements are strongly supported by finite sets, i.e., whenever a permutation fixes an element
x
of the set then it has to fix the atoms occurring in
x
pointwise.
nominal sets). Soundness with respect to the standard nominal set models was proved only for a restricted calculus in
[
8
]
.

Contributions.

The system presented in this paper is a generalisation of the latter in two ways: judgements here can include arbitrary fixed point constraints in contexts, and equational axioms are taken into account, making the system more expressive while maintaining soundness for standard nominal set models. Devising a proof system for general fixed point judgements that is sound and complete is challenging; the difficulty lies in the variable rule, which needs to be sufficiently powerful to derive all the valid judgements, but only those that are valid in all nominal set models. A key idea to obtain soundness and completeness with respect to standard nominal set semantics is to analyse the permutation cycles in fixed-point assumptions.

We also study satisfiability of equality constraints: we show that nominal unification is decidable and unitary if
E=\emptyset
, and it is finitary if
\mathtt{C}
ommutativity axioms are included. More precisely, we provide a unification algorithm specified as a set of simplification rules on unification problems, which we show terminating
and correct.
The normal forms of the simplification system consist of a finite set (
\Upsilon
) of fixed-point constraints and a substitution. The algorithm outputs a most general solution of the form
(\Upsilon,\sigma)
if
E=\emptyset
and a finite complete set of solutions of the form
(\Upsilon_{i},\sigma_{i})
if
E=\mathtt{C}
. Having a finitary theory is important in practice, for example to design programming languages and theorem provers that support binders and equational axioms, to specify completion procedures for rewriting modulo
\alpha
and
E
, etc.
A notable feature of our unification algorithm is its treatment of computed
substitutions
: it ensures that meta-variables are instantiated while maintaining stability under renaming of bound names—preserving consistency when fresh names are introduced during computation (see Remark
1
).

Proofs of selected results can be found in the Appendix. An extended version with all the proofs is available in
[
9
]
.

2
Preliminaries

In this section, we introduce some basic notions and notations used throughout the paper; for more details we refer the reader to
[
2
,
19
,
15
]
.

Nominal Syntax.

Fix disjoint countably infinite sets
\mathbb{A}=\{a,b,c,\ldots\}
of
atoms
and
\mathbb{V}=\{X,Y,Z,\ldots\}
of
variables
. A
signature

\Sigma
is a set of
term-formers
(
\mathtt{f},\mathtt{g},\ldots
)
-
disjoint from
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{V}

-
where each
\mathtt{f}\in\Sigma
has a fixed
arity

n\geq 0
, denoted
\mathtt{f}:n
.
A
finite
permutation
\pi
of atoms is a bijection
\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{A}
such that the set
\mathtt{dom}(\pi):=\{a\in\mathbb{A}\mid\pi(a)\neq a\}
is finite. Write
\mathtt{id}
for the
identity permutation
,
\pi\circ\pi^{\prime}
for the
composition
of
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
, and
\pi^{-1}
for the
inverse
of
\pi
.
We use
swappings
of atoms of the form
(a\ b)
to implement the renaming of atoms, for the purpose of
\alpha
-conversion. Given two permutations
\pi
and
\rho
, the permutation
\pi^{\rho}=\rho\circ\pi\circ\rho^{-1}
denotes the
conjugate
of
\pi
with respect to
\rho
.

Nominal terms
are defined inductively by the following grammar:

s,t,u::=a\mid\pi\cdot X\mid\mathtt{f^{E}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\mid[a]t

where
a
is an
atom
;
\pi\cdot X
is a
suspension
, where
\pi
is an atom permutation. Intuitively,
\pi\cdot X
denotes that
X
will get substituted for a term and then
\pi
will permute the atoms in that term accordingly (i.e., the permutation
\pi
is suspended). Suspensions of the form
\mathtt{id}\cdot X
will be represented simply by
X
;
a function symbol

\mathtt{f}:n
of arity
n
may be equipped with an equational theory
\mathtt{E}
, hence
\mathtt{f^{E}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
denotes the
application
of
\mathtt{f^{E}}
to a tuple
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
. We write
\mathtt{f^{\emptyset}}
, or simply
\mathtt{f}
, to emphasise that no equational theory is assumed for
\mathtt{f}
;
[a]t
denotes the
abstraction
of the atom
a
over the term
t
. The set of all nominal terms formed from a signature
\Sigma
will be denoted by
\mathbb{F}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})
.

Write
\mathtt{Var}(-)
and
\mathtt{Atm}(-)
to denote the sets of all variables and all atoms occurring in the argument, respectively. A nominal term
t
is
ground
when it does not mention variables, i.e., when
\mathtt{Var}(t)=\emptyset
. The set of all ground terms will be denoted by
\mathbb{F}(\Sigma)
. The set of
free atoms
of a ground term
g
is denoted as
\mathtt{fn}(t)
and consists of the atoms, say
a
, that do not occur under the scope of an abstraction
[a]
, that is,
\mathtt{fn}(a)=\{a\},\mathtt{fn}(\mathtt{f^{E}}(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}))=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathtt{fn}(g_{i}),
and
\mathtt{fn}([a]g)=\mathtt{fn}(g)\setminus\{a\}
. Write
t\equiv u
for
syntactic identity
of terms.

The
object-level permutation action on terms
, denoted as
\pi\cdot t
, is defined via the homomorphic application of a permutation on the structure of the term:
\pi\cdot a\equiv\pi(a),~\pi\cdot(\pi^{\prime}\cdot X)\equiv(\pi\circ\pi^{\prime})\cdot X,\pi\cdot([a]t)\equiv[\pi(a)](\pi\cdot t)
and
\pi\cdot\mathtt{f^{E}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\equiv\mathtt{f^{E}}(\pi\cdot t_{1},\ldots,\pi\cdot t_{n})
.

Substitutions
, usually denoted by
\sigma,\delta,\ldots
, are finite mappings from variables to terms, which extend homomorphically over terms. We write
Id
to denote the identity substitution,
\mathtt{dom}(\sigma)

to denote the domain
of
\sigma
. Substitutions and permutations commute:
\pi\cdot(s\sigma)\equiv(\pi\cdot s)\sigma
.

Example 1

Abstractions will be used to represent binding operators (e.g. the lambda operator from the
\lambda
-calculus). Considering the signature
\Sigma=\{\mathtt{lam}:1,\mathtt{app}:2\}
and treating
\lambda
-variables as atoms,
\lambda
-terms can be inductively generated by the grammar:

e::=a\mid\mathtt{app}(e,e)\mid\mathtt{lam}([a]e)
.
Note that
(a\ b)\cdot\mathtt{lam}([a]a)\equiv\mathtt{lam}([b]b)
implements the renaming of
a
for
b
using the swapping
(a\ {b})
.

Groups and Permutations.

For completeness, we present some elementary notions, notations and properties of groups and permutations that will be used throughout the paper. This subsection can be skipped on a first reading and consulted later as a reference.
A
permutation group

is a group
2
2

2

A
group
is a set
G
equipped with an operation
\circ:G\times G\to G
that satisfies four properties: closure, associativity, identity, and invertibility.

G
whose elements are permutations on a set and the group operation is the composition
\circ
of permutations.
If
S
is a non-empty subset of a permutation group
G
, then we denote by
\left\langle S\right\rangle
the subgroup of all elements of

G
that can be expressed as the finite composition of elements in
S
and their inverses.

\left\langle S\right\rangle
is called the
subgroup generated by
S
.
We will work with the group of
finite
permutations of atoms
\mathbb{A}
, denoted by
{\mathcal{G}}\left(\mathbb{A}\right)
. Moreover, given
B\subseteq\mathbb{A}
write
{\mathcal{G}}\left(B\right)=\{\pi\in{\mathcal{G}}\left(\mathbb{A}\right){}\mid\mathtt{dom}(\pi)\subseteq B\}
.
Recall that finite permutations can be expressed in two equivalent ways: either as compositions of swapping (a.k.a. transpositions), or as compositions of
disjoint permutation cycles
. E.g.,
(a\ d)(a\ c)(a\ b)(e\ f)
and
(a\ b\ c\ d)(e\ f)
represent the same permutation.
If
H
and
K
are subsets of a permutation group
G
whose operation is
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\circ}
(in particular, if
H
and
K
are subgroups of
G
), the set
\{h{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\circ}k\mid h\in H,k\in K\}
will be denoted by
H{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\circ}K

3
3

3

The usual notation is just by juxtaposition
HK
, we will distinguish the operator
H{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\circ}K
to improve readability later on.
.
In general,
H{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\circ}K
is not a subgroup of
G
, even when
H
and
K
are subgroups of
G
. However,
H{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\circ}K
is a subgroup of
G
iff
H{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\circ}K=K{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\circ}H
.

Nominal Sets.

A
{\mathcal{G}}\left(\mathbb{A}\right)
-set
, denoted by
\mathscr{X}
, is a pair
(|\mathscr{X}|,\cdot)
consisting of an
underlying set

|\mathscr{X}|
and a
permutation action

\cdot
, which is a group action on
|\mathscr{X}|
, i.e., an operation

\cdot:{\mathcal{G}}\left(\mathbb{A}\right)\times|\mathscr{X}|\to|\mathscr{X}|
such that
\mathtt{id}\cdot x=x
and
\pi\cdot(\pi^{\prime}\cdot x)=(\pi\circ\pi^{\prime})\cdot x
, for every
x\in|\mathscr{X}|
and
\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}\left(\mathbb{A}\right)
. We will write
\pi\cdot_{\mathscr{X}}x
, when we want to make
\mathscr{X}
clear.
For
B\subseteq\mathbb{A}
, we denote
{\tt pfix}(B)
as the set of permutations that fix pointwise the elements of
B
, i.e.

{\tt pfix}(B)=\{\pi\in{\mathcal{G}}\left(\mathbb{A}\right)\mid\forall a\in B.~\pi(a)=a\}.

A set
B\subseteq\mathbb{A}

supports
an element
x\in|\mathscr{X}|
when for all permutations
\pi\in{\mathcal{G}}\left(\mathbb{A}\right)
:

\pi\in{\tt pfix}(B)\implies\pi\cdot x=x
.
Additionally,
B

strongly supports

x\in|\mathscr{X}|
if the reverse implication holds.
A
nominal set
is a
{\mathcal{G}}\left(\mathbb{A}\right)
-set
\mathscr{X}
all of whose elements are finitely supported.

\mathscr{X,Y,Z}
will range over nominal sets.

The
support
of an element
x\in|\mathscr{X}|
of a nominal set
\mathscr{X}
is defined as

\mathtt{supp}(x)=\bigcap\{B\mid\text{$B$ is finite and supports $x$}\}.

This implies that
\mathtt{supp}(x)
is the
least
finite support of
x
.
The support provides a semantics for freshness constraints in the class of nominal sets
[
15
,
18
]
:
When

a\notin\mathtt{supp}(x)
,
we write

a\#_{\tt sem}x
, and read this as “
a
is fresh for
x
”.

One of the main properties of the relation
a\#_{\tt sem}x
is the so-called
Choose-a-Fresh-Name Principle
, which says that if
x\in|\mathscr{X}|
has a finite support, then it is always possible to choose a new atom
a
such that
a\#_{\tt sem}x
. Therefore, the notion of freshness provides a generative aspect of fresh atoms.

Example 2
(Some simple nominal sets)

The
{\mathcal{G}}\left(\mathbb{A}\right)
-set
(\mathbb{A},\cdot)
with the action
\pi\cdot_{\mathbb{A}}a=\pi(a)
is a nominal set and
\mathtt{supp}(a)=\{a\}
. The group
{\mathcal{G}}\left(\mathbb{A}\right)
equipped with the action by conjugation
\rho\cdot_{{\mathcal{G}}\left(\mathbb{A}\right)}\pi=\pi^{\rho}
forms a nominal set and
\mathtt{supp}(\pi)=\mathtt{dom}(\pi)
. The set of ground terms
\mathbb{F}(\Sigma)
with the usual permutation action on terms is a nominal set and
\mathtt{supp}(g)=\mathtt{Atm}(g)
.
Another example is the set
\mathcal{P}_{\tt fin}(\mathbb{A})=\{B\subset\mathbb{A}\mid B\text{ is finite}\}
. Then the
{\mathcal{G}}\left(\mathbb{A}\right)
-set
(\mathcal{P}_{\tt fin}(\mathbb{A}),\cdot)
with the action
\pi\cdot_{\mathcal{P}_{\tt fin}(\mathbb{A})}B=\{\pi\cdot_{\mathbb{A}}a\mid a\in B\}
is a nominal set and
\mathtt{supp}(B)=B
.

The new

\mathsf{N}

-quantifier.

The

\mathsf{N}

-quantifier
[
19
]
models the quantification used when we informally write “rename
x
in
\lambda x.t
to a new name”. Thus, if
\phi
is the description of some
property of atomic names
a
, then
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}a}.\phi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}a})
holds when
\phi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}a})
holds for all but finitely many atoms
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}a}
. Usually,
\phi
is a predicate in higher-order logic
[
2
]
or in ZFA or FM set theory
[
19
,
16
]
. The

\mathsf{N}

-quantifier is defined as

\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}a}.\phi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}a})\text{ holds iff }\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}a}\in\mathbb{A}\mid\phi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}a})\text{ holds}\}\text{ is cofinite}.

(1)

Therefore,
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}a}.(\ldots)
means “for a cofinite number of atoms
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}a},(\ldots)
” . The quantifier

\mathsf{N}

allows to quantify the
generative
aspect of names/atoms. This aligns with the Choose-a-Fresh-Name Principle of the relation
\#_{\tt sem}
. The connection between these two notions is established by
Pitts’ equivalence

[
19
,
2
]
, which defines the freshness of an atom
a
for an element
x
in a nominal set using the new-quantifier

\mathsf{N}

and a fixed-point identity as follows:

a\#_{\tt sem}x\iff\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}.(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c})\cdot x=x.

(2)

The following equivalences will be heavily used throughout the text:

\displaystyle a\notin\mathtt{supp}(x)

\displaystyle\iff a\#_{\tt sem}x

(3)

\displaystyle\iff\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}.(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c})\cdot x=x\text{ holds}

\displaystyle\iff\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}\in\mathbb{A}\mid(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c})\cdot x=x\}\text{ is cofinite.}

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{n}}
be list of atoms. We will write
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.\phi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}})
as shorthand for
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}.\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{2}}.\ldots\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{n}}.\phi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}})
. Since
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}a_{1}}.\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}a_{2}}.\phi
holds if and only if
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}a_{2}}.\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}a_{1}}.\phi
holds, then the set
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}
can be interchangeably treated as either an unordered list or a finite set of atoms. We will refer to this set as the set of

\mathsf{N}

-quantified names
.

3
Generalised Permutation Fixed-Points in Nominal Sets

In this section, we consider
generalised fixed-point identities
: a version of fixed-point identities with general

\mathsf{N}

-quantified atom permutations
\pi
.
That is,

\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.\pi\cdot x=x,

where
\pi
is a finite composition of disjoint cycles, and not only swappings. For example,
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{3}}.(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{2}}})\circ(b\ {d})\cdot x=x
is a generalised fixed-point identity.

In addition, we will split the permutation
\pi
into
\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
, consisting of the cycles that contain at least one

\mathsf{N}

-quantified atom, and
\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
, consisting of cycles whose atoms are not quantified.

Example 3

Consider the generalised fixed-point identity
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}.\pi\cdot x=x
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{3}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{4}}
and

\pi=(a_{1}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{2}})\circ(a_{2}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{3}})\circ\hbox{\pagecolor{red!10}$(a_{3}\ a_{4}\ a_{5})$}\circ(a_{6}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{4}})\circ\hbox{\pagecolor{red!10}$(a_{7}\ a_{8})$}
. Note that
\pi

is equivalent to the permutation consisting of the composition of
\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
and
\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
:

\pi=\underbrace{(a_{1}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{2}})\circ(a_{2}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{3}})\circ(a_{6}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{4}})}_{\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}}\circ\underbrace{\hbox{\pagecolor{red!10}$(a_{3}\ a_{4}\ a_{5})$}\circ\hbox{\pagecolor{red!10}$(a_{7}\ a_{8})$}}_{\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}}.

Interesting permutations with different behaviours are: (i)
\pi_{1}=(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})\circ(b\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{2}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{3}}\ d)\circ(f\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{4}})
, with
(\pi_{1})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}=\pi_{1}
and
(\pi_{1})_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}=\mathtt{id}
; and (ii)
\pi_{2}=(a_{1}\ b_{1}\ d_{1})\circ(a_{2}\ b_{2})
with
(\pi_{2})_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}=\pi_{2}
and
(\pi_{2})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}=\mathtt{id}
.

We formalise this decomposition of permutations in the following definition.

Definition 1
(Classifying Permutation Cycles)

Let
\pi
be a permutation and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}
a finite set of

\mathsf{N}

-quantified atoms. Define
\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
as the product of disjoint cycles of
\pi
that mention atoms in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}
, and
\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
as the product of disjoint cycles of
\pi
that do not mention atoms in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}
. Define
\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}:=\mathtt{id}
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}\cap\mathtt{dom}(\pi)=\emptyset
. Similarly,
\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}:=\mathtt{id}
if every disjoint cycle of
\pi
mentions at least one atom from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}
.

Example 4

The following illustrate some properties of splitting permutations:

1.

In general, it is not true that
(\pi_{1}\circ\pi_{2})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}=(\pi_{1})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\circ(\pi_{2})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
for all permutations
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
. Take
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}
and the permutations

\pi_{1}=(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})

and

\pi_{2}=(a\ b\ d)
. Then

\pi_{1}\circ\pi_{2}=(a\ b\ d\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})\neq(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})=(\pi_{1})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}}\circ(\pi_{2})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}}.

2.

In general, it is not true that
(\pi^{\rho})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}=(\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}})^{\rho}
, for all permutations
\pi
and
\rho
. Similarly,
(\pi^{\rho})_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}=((\pi)_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}})^{\rho}
does not hold in general. Take
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}
and consider

\pi=(a\ b\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})\circ(d\ e)
and
\rho=({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}\ d)
.
After computing the conjugate and reorganising the cycles we obtain

(\pi^{\rho})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}}=({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}\ e)\neq(a\ b\ d)=(\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}})^{\rho}
. We can similarly check that
(\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}})^{\rho}\neq(\pi^{\rho})_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}}
.

Assuming permutations are written as compositions of disjoint cycles, the next lemma states that if
\pi\cdot x=x
and
\eta
is a cycle in
\pi
, then if one atom of
\eta
is not in
\mathtt{supp}(x)
, none of the atoms in
\eta
are in
\mathtt{supp}(x)
. Moreover, it also states that all the permutations in the group generated by permutations that fix an element
x
of a nominal set, also fix
x
.

Lemma 1

Let
x
be an element of a nominal set and
\pi,\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n}
be permutations written as compositions of disjoint cycles.

1.

If
\pi\cdot x=x
,
\eta
is a cycle of
\pi
and
\mathtt{dom}(\eta)-\mathtt{supp}(x)\neq\emptyset

then
\mathtt{dom}(\eta)\cap\mathtt{supp}(x)=\emptyset
.

2.

If
\eta_{i}\cdot x=x
, for all
i=1,\ldots,n
, and
\rho\in\left\langle\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n}\right\rangle
, then
\rho\cdot x=x
.

For e.g., if
(a_{1}\ a_{2}\ {a_{3}})\circ(a_{4}\ {a_{5}})\cdot x=x
and
a_{2}\notin\mathtt{supp}(x)
, then
a_{3},a_{1}\notin\mathtt{supp}(x)
.

The following result generalises Pitts’ equivalence (
2
) to any permutation
\pi
.

Lemma 2
(Generalised Pitts’ equivalence)

1.

\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.~\pi\cdot x=x
iff
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\cdot x=x
and
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\cdot x=x
.

2.

\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.~\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\cdot x=x
iff for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}\cap\mathtt{supp}(x)=\emptyset
implies
\mathtt{dom}(\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}})\cap\mathtt{supp}(x)=\emptyset
.

3.

\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.~\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\cdot x=x
iff for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}\cap\mathtt{supp}(x)=\emptyset
implies
\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\cdot x=x
.

4
A proof system

In this section, we introduce notions of fixed-point constraint, equality constraint and
\alpha,\mathtt{C}
-equivalence judgement, together with derivation rules (Figure
1
). Our judgements and rules are more general than the ones in
[
18
,
8
,
5
]
since we consider
general

\mathsf{N}

-quantified permutations subsuming both freshness and algebraic (here, commutative) conditions (more discussion about expressivity can be found below); as a consequence, we need only one predicate (equality, denoted
\approx_{\tt C}
) and only one set of rules to handle notions of term equality and name freshness. Before discussing the rules, we define generalised fixed-point contexts and judgements, as well as a normalisation process to refine permutations in contexts.

Definition 2
(Generalised fixed-point constraint and context)

1.

An
\alpha,\mathtt{C}
-equality constraint
(or simply an
equality constraint
) is an expression of the form
t\approx_{\tt C}u
, where
t
and
u
are nominal terms.

2.

A (generalised)
fixed-point constraint
is an
\alpha,\mathtt{C}
-equality constraint of the form
\pi\cdot t\approx_{\tt C}t
, where
\pi
is a permutation. A fixed-point constraint of the form
\pi\cdot X\approx_{\tt C}X
is called
primitive
.

3.

A (generalised)
fixed-point context
is an expression of the form
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.\Upsilon
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{n}}
and
\Upsilon
is a finite set containing only primitive fixed-point constraints. For simplicity, we write
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
to denote
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.\Upsilon
. Symbols such as
\Upsilon,\Psi,\Upsilon^{\prime},\Psi^{\prime}
, are used to represent fixed-point contexts.

We will adopt the existing notation for fixed-point constraints
[
5
]
and abbreviate
\pi\cdot t\approx_{\tt C}t
as
\pi\curlywedge_{\tt C}t
, writing primitive constraints as
\pi\curlywedge_{\tt C}X
.

Definition 3
(Judgement)

An
\alpha,\mathtt{C}
-equivalence judgment
is an expression of the form
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.(\Upsilon\vdash t\approx_{\tt C}u)

consisting of a fixed-point context
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}^{\prime}}}
, with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}^{\prime}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}
, and an equality constraint
t\approx_{\tt C}u
, which may contain atoms from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}
. We simplify the notation to
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash t\approx_{\tt C}u
.

We write
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}|_{X}
to refer to the fixed-point constraints on the variable
X
, that is,
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}|_{X}:=\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.\{\pi\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\mid\pi\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\in\Upsilon\}
. We extend the notation
\mathtt{Atm}(-)
to contexts
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
. For example,
\mathtt{Atm}(\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}.\{(a\ b\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c})\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\})=\{a,b,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}\}
. The set also includes vacuous

\mathsf{N}

-quantified names, as in
\mathtt{Atm}(\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c,c_{1}}.\{(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c})\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\})=\{a,b,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c,c_{1}}\}
. We may slightly abuse notation when convenient and write
\pi\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\in\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
instead of
\pi\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\in\Upsilon
.

Expressivity.

In the standard nominal approach using freshness constraints, contexts store only information about the freshness of atoms in (meta-)variables — they impose conditions on which atoms cannot occur free in any interpretation of the variables. Generalised fixed-point contexts are more expressive.
Consider the context
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}.\Psi_{1}=\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}.\{(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}\ b)\circ(d\ e)\curlywedge_{\tt C}Z\}
. By the generalized Pitts’ equivalence (see Lemma
2
), this context implies two things:

1.

The atoms
a,b
(and also
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}
) cannot occur freely in an interpretation of
Z
.

2.

(d\ e)\curlywedge_{\tt C}Z
holds, which does not force the atoms
d
and
e
to be fresh in interpretations of
Z
, since neither
d
nor
e
are

\mathsf{N}

-quantified or occur in a cycle with a

\mathsf{N}

-quantified name. For example,
Z
could be mapped to the term
d+e
where
+
is a commutative operator. In this case,
a,b
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}
do not occur freely in
d+e
, while
d
and
e
occur freely in
d+e
, but
(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}\ b)\cdot(d+e)\approx_{\tt C}d+e
and
(d\ e)\cdot(d+e)\approx_{\tt C}d+e
.

The example above shows that generalised fixed-point contexts are a form of “hybrid context”: they store more than just freshness information; they also encode relevant fixed-point constraints that cannot be reduced to freshness.

4.1
Normalisation

We can explore the properties of permutations to improve our fixed-point contexts. The idea is to use the properties in Lemma
1
to transform contexts into equivalent, simpler ones, using first the rule
(R1)
exhaustively and then
(R2)
:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\textbf{(R1)}&\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.(\{\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\circ\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\}\cup\Upsilon^{\prime})\implies\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.(\{\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\curlywedge_{\tt C}X,\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\}\cup\Upsilon^{\prime})\\[2.84526pt]
\textbf{(R2)}&\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.(\{\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\curlywedge_{\tt C}X,(\rho_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\circ\rho^{\prime}_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}})\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\}\cup\Upsilon^{\prime})\implies\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.(\{(\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\circ\rho_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}})\curlywedge_{\tt C}X,\rho^{\prime}_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\}\cup\Upsilon^{\prime})\\
&\text{if }\mathtt{dom}(\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}})\cap\mathtt{dom}(\rho_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}})\neq\emptyset\text{, where $\rho$ is a cycle disjoint from $\rho^{\prime}$.}\end{array}

We justify our rules as follows: For rule
(R1)
: From Definition
1
, it follows that given
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}
, every permutation
\pi
that is written as a product of disjoint cycles can be rewritten such that
\pi=\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\circ\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
. By Lemma
2
, the constraint
\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\circ\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\curlywedge_{\tt C}X
is equivalent to
\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\wedge\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\curlywedge_{\tt C}X
. For rule
(R2)
the constraints on the lhs are normal forms w.r.t. (
R1
): From the constraint
\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\curlywedge_{\tt C}X
it follows that the atoms in
\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
cannot occur free in the instances of
X
(they are not in the support of the instances of
X
). Since
\rho
is a cycle that contains atoms in
\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
, by Lemma
1
, all the atoms in the cycle must not be in the support of
X
. From the equivalences in (
3
), not being in the support
is equivalent to being the fixed-point with atoms in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}
. The composition
\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\circ\rho_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
generates a new permutation
(\pi^{\prime})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
with the atoms of
\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
and
\rho_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
organised as a composition of disjoint cycles.

Example 5

To illustrate (
R1
), consider the context
\Psi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}}=\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}.\{((a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c})\circ(d\ {e})\curlywedge_{\tt C}Z\}
which is equivalent to the context
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}.\{(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c})\curlywedge_{\tt C}Z,(d\ {e})\curlywedge_{\tt C}Z\}
with simpler constraints. The left constraint gives information about freshness whereas the right constraint is about terms that are fixed by permutation
(d\ {e})
. To illustrate (
R2
), consider the context
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}.\Psi_{2}=\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}.\{(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c})\curlywedge_{\tt C}X,(a\ b)\circ(d\ e)\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\}
. By generalised Pitts’ equivalence (Lemma
2
),
(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c})\curlywedge_{\tt C}X
alone implies that
a
cannot occur freely in any interpretation of
X
, as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}
is

\mathsf{N}

-quantified. In contrast, when considering the constraint
(a\ b)\circ(d\ e)\curlywedge_{\tt C}X
on its own, the names
a,b,d,e
may occur freely in an interpretation of
X
. However, when the two constraints are considered together, as in
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}.\Psi_{2}
, the scenario for the name
b
changes: it follows from Lemma
1
that
b
also cannot be free in any interpretation of
X
.
Rule (
R2
) composes both permutations as in
(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c})\circ(a\ {b})
obtaining the equivalent context
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}.\{(a\ b\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c})\curlywedge_{\tt C}X,(d\ e)\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\}
where it is explicit that
a
and
b
must be fresh in all the instances of
X
.

Important Assumption.

From now on, unless explicitly stated otherwise, we will assume that
every fixed-point context, say
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
, is in normal form with respect to the exhaustive application of rules (
R1
) and (
R2
)
.

Thus, just as we can split a permutation
\pi
into its disjoint parts
\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
and
\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
, we will split a generalised fixed-point context
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
into its “freshness” part (denoted
(\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}})_{\#}
) and “pure fixed-point” (but non-freshness) part (denoted
(\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}})_{\curlywedge_{\tt C}}
). That is,
(\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}})
will be divided into the following disjoint sets:

(\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}})_{\#}:=\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.\{\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\mid\pi\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\in\Upsilon\}
and
(\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}})_{\curlywedge_{\tt C}}:=\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.\{\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\mid\pi\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\in\Upsilon\}
.
For example, the context
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.\Psi_{2}
from Example
5
will be split into
(\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}.\Psi_{2})_{\#}=\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}.\{(a\ b\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c})\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\}
and
(\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}.\Psi_{2})_{\curlywedge_{\tt C}}=\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}.\{(d\ e)\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\}
.

In the following we use the notation

\mathtt{perm}((\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}})_{\curlywedge_{\tt C}}):=\{\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\mid\pi\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\in\Upsilon\}

4.2
Derivation rules for
\approx_{\tt C}

\frac{\hskip 18.49988pt}{\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash a\approx_{\tt C}a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=$({{\approx}}_{\tt C}\,\mathbf{a})$}\frac{\pi^{\prime-1}\circ\pi\in\mathtt{P}_{\scalebox{0.5}{$$}}(\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}|_{X})}{\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash\pi\cdot X\approx_{\tt C}\pi^{\prime}\cdot X}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=$({{\approx}}_{\tt C}\,\mathbf{var})$}\frac{\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash t_{1}\approx_{\tt C}t_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\ldots\hskip 9.24994pt\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash t_{n}\approx_{\tt C}t_{n}^{\prime}}{\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash{\tt f}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\approx_{\tt C}{\tt f}(t_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,t_{n}^{\prime})}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=$\mathtt{f}\neq\mathtt{f^{C}}\ ({{\approx}}_{\tt C}\,\mathbf{\mathtt{f}})$}\frac{\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash s_{0}\approx_{\tt C}t_{i}\hskip 9.24994pt\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash s_{1}\approx_{\tt C}t_{1-i}\hskip 9.24994pti=0,1}{\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash\mathtt{f^{C}}(s_{0},s_{1})\approx_{\tt C}\mathtt{f^{C}}(t_{0},t_{1})}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=$({{\approx}}_{\tt C}\,\mathbf{\mathtt{f^{C}}})$}\frac{\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash t\approx_{\tt C}t^{\prime}}{\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash[a]t\approx_{\tt C}[a]t^{\prime}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=$({{\approx}}_{\tt C}\,\mathbf{[a]})$}\frac{\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}}\vdash(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})\cdot t\approx_{\tt C}(b\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})\cdot s}{\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash[a]t\approx_{\tt C}[b]s}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=$({{\approx}}_{\tt C}\,\mathbf{ab})$}

Figure 1:
Derivation rules for
\approx_{\tt C}
.

We are now ready to present our derivation rules (Figure
1
). The rule
({{\approx}}_{\tt C}\,\mathbf{ab})
for abstraction relies on Pitts’ definition of
\alpha
-equivalence on nominal sets (
=_{\alpha}
) using the

\mathsf{N}

-quantifier
[
2
]
:

(a_{1},x_{1})=_{\alpha}(a_{2},x_{2})\iff\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}.~(a_{1}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})\cdot x_{1}=(a_{2}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})\cdot x_{2}.

(4)

This means that two abstractions
[a_{1}]t
and
[a_{2}]s
are equal if
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}.(a_{1}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})\cdot t=(a_{2}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})\cdot s
. As expected, the rule for commutativity
({{\approx}}_{\tt C}\,\mathbf{\mathtt{f^{C}}})
ensures that to prove
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash{\mathtt{f}}^{\mathtt{C}}(s_{0},s_{1})\approx_{\tt C}{\mathtt{f}}^{\mathtt{C}}(t_{0},t_{1})
there must exist derivations of
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash s_{0}\approx_{\tt C}t_{0}
and
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash s_{1}\approx_{\tt C}t_{1}
; or derivations of
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash s_{0}\approx_{\tt C}t_{1}
and
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash s_{1}\approx_{\tt C}t_{0}
.

The rule
({{\approx}}_{\tt C}\,\mathbf{var})
for handling variables is more involved. It is inspired by Lemma
1
and Lemma
2
.
Applying
\pi^{\prime-1}
to both sides of
\pi\cdot X\approx_{\tt C}\pi^{\prime}\cdot X
yields
\pi^{\prime-1}\cdot(\pi\cdot X)\approx_{\tt C}X
, which simplifies to
(\pi^{\prime-1}\circ\pi)\cdot X\approx_{\tt C}X
. At this point, it is essential to verify whether
\pi^{\prime-1}\circ\pi
belongs to the
group generated by permutations derived from the context
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}|_{X}
, denoted as
\mathtt{P}_{\scalebox{0.5}{$$}}(\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}|_{X})
, whose permutations are known to fix
X
. Here

\mathtt{P}_{\scalebox{0.5}{$$}}(\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}|_{X})={\mathcal{G}}\left(\mathtt{Atm}((\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}|_{X})_{\#})\right){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\circ}\left\langle\mathtt{perm}((\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}|_{X})_{\curlywedge_{\tt C}})\right\rangle,

(5)

which is a subgroup of
{\mathcal{G}}\left(\mathbb{A}\right)
because
\rho_{1}\circ\rho_{2}=\rho_{2}\circ\rho_{1}
holds for all
\rho_{1}\in{\mathcal{G}}\left(\mathtt{Atm}((\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}|_{X})_{\#})\right)
and all
\rho_{2}\in\left\langle\mathtt{perm}((\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}|_{X})_{\curlywedge_{\tt C}})\right\rangle
since
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
are disjoint.
The remaining rules are standard and rely on the decomposition of the constraint over the structure of its terms.

Example 6

The judgement
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}.\{(d\ e\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c})\curlywedge_{\tt C}X,(a\ b)\curlywedge_{\tt C}Y\}\vdash\mathtt{f^{C}}([d]X,((a\ b)\cdot Y)\approx_{\tt C}\mathtt{f^{C}}(Y,[e]X)
is derivable. For
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}}=\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}.\{(d\ e\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c})\curlywedge_{\tt C}X,(a\ b)\curlywedge_{\tt C}Y\}
, we have the following derivation:

(a\ b)\in{\mathcal{G}}\left(\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}\}\right){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\circ}\left\langle(a\ b)\right\rangle

(a\ b)\in\mathtt{P}_{\scalebox{0.5}{$$}}(\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}}|_{Y})

({{\approx}}_{\tt C}\,\mathbf{var})

\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}}\vdash(a\ b)\cdot Y\approx_{\tt C}Y

(d\ e\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})\in{\mathcal{G}}\left(\{d,e,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}\}\right){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\circ}\left\langle\emptyset\right\rangle

(d\ e\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})\in\mathtt{P}_{\scalebox{0.5}{$$}}(\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}}|_{X})

({{\approx}}_{\tt C}\,\mathbf{var})

\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}}\vdash(d\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})\cdot X\approx_{\tt C}(e\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})\cdot X

({{\approx}}_{\tt C}\,\mathbf{ab})

\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}}\vdash[d]X\approx_{\tt C}[e]X

({{\approx}}_{\tt C}\,\mathbf{\mathtt{f^{C}}})

\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}}\vdash\mathtt{f^{C}}([d]X,(a\ b)\cdot Y)\approx_{\tt C}\mathtt{f^{C}}(Y,[e]X)

While the freshness approach
[
24
]
requires
a\#Y
and
b\#Y
to derive
(a\ b)\cdot Y\approx_{\tt C}Y
, the fixed-point calculus removes this limitation. Here,
(a\ b)\cdot Y\approx_{\tt C}Y
is derivable without relying on any freshness assumption. Yet, if desired, it could be derived using freshness; for instance, it could be derived using the context
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}.\{(a\ b\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c})\curlywedge_{\tt C}Y\}
. This shows that the fixed-point calculus generalises and extends the freshness calculus, establishing it as proper subcalculus.

4.3
Properties

Our calculus satisfies essential structural properties, as summarised in Theorem
2
. It upholds
equivariance
, ensuring that derivations remain invariant under the action of permutations, thereby preserving consistency across transformations. It also satisfies
inversion
, a useful property for inductive proofs on derivations, as it facilitates the bottom-up application of derivation rules. More significantly, and in contrast with previous fixed-point approaches
[
5
,
8
]
, our framework enables the definition of a sound and complete semantics for commutative nominal algebra with

\mathsf{N}

-quantified fixed-point constraints in nominal sets, as established in Theorem
4.2
below. The key insight to achieve this result lies in the
({{\approx}}_{\tt C}\,\mathbf{var})
rule, more precisely in the construction of the group
\mathtt{P}_{\scalebox{0.5}{$$}}(\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}|_{X})
(see (
5
)), which carefully distinguishes freshness information from fixed-point behaviour.

Theorem 4.1
(Miscellaneous)

The following hold:

1.

(Inversion) The derivation rules from Figure
1
are invertible.

2.

(Equivariance)
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash s\approx_{\tt C}t
iff
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash\rho\cdot s\approx_{\tt C}\rho\cdot t
,
where
\rho
is an arbitrary permutation (it could have atoms of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}
in its domain).

3.

(Equivalence)
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash-\approx_{\tt C}-
is an equivalence relation.

4.

(Strengthening) If
(\Upsilon\uplus\{\pi\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash s\approx_{\tt C}t
and
(\mathtt{dom}(\pi)\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}})\cap\mathtt{Atm}(s,t)=\emptyset
, then
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash s\approx_{\tt C}t
.

Soundness and Completeness.

We first define the semantics of judgements. Below, we introduce the fundamental notions required to present our results, while the full construction of the semantic model is provided in the appendix.

Given a signature
\Sigma
, which may contain commutative function symbols, a
commutative nominal
\Sigma
-algebra

\mathfrak{A}
(or just
\Sigma
-algebra) consists of:

1.

A nominal set
\mathscr{A}=(|\mathscr{A}|,\cdot)

-
the domain.

2.

An injective equivariant
5
5

5

Given
{\mathcal{G}}\left(\mathbb{A}\right)
-sets
\mathscr{X,Y}
, call a map
f:|\mathscr{X}|\to|\mathscr{Y}|

equivariant
when
\pi\cdot f(x)=f(\pi\cdot x)
for all
\pi\in{\mathcal{G}}\left(\mathbb{A}\right)
and
x\in|\mathscr{X}|
.
map
\mathtt{atom}^{\mathfrak{A}}:\mathbb{A}\to|\mathscr{A}|
to interpret atoms; we write the interpretation
\mathtt{atom}(a)
as
a^{\mathfrak{A}}\in|\mathscr{A}|
.

3.

An equivariant map
\mathtt{abs}^{\mathfrak{A}}:\mathbb{A}\times|\mathscr{A}|\to|\mathscr{A}|
such that
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}.(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c})\cdot\mathtt{abs}^{\mathfrak{A}}(a,x)=\mathtt{abs}^{\mathfrak{A}}(a,x)
, for all
a\in\mathbb{A}
and
x\in|\mathscr{A}|
; we use this to interpret abstraction.

4.

An equivariant map
f^{\mathfrak{A}}:|\mathscr{A}|^{n}\to|\mathscr{A}|
, for each
\mathtt{f}:n
in
\Sigma
.

5.

For each
\mathtt{f^{C}}
in
\Sigma
, the associated equivariant map
f^{\mathtt{C},\mathfrak{A}}
satisfies
f^{\mathtt{C},\mathfrak{A}}(x_{1},x_{2})=f^{\mathtt{C},\mathfrak{A}}(x_{2},x_{1})
for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in|\mathscr{A}|
. Sometimes,
\mathfrak{A}
will be called a
nominal model of
\mathtt{C}
(over
\Sigma
)
.

We use
\mathfrak{A,B,C}
to denote
\Sigma
-algebras.

A
valuation

\varsigma
in a commutative
\Sigma
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
maps unknowns
X\in\mathbb{V}
to elements

\varsigma(X)\in|\mathscr{A}|
. Below we define an equivariant function
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{A}}_{\varsigma}:\mathbb{F}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})\to|\mathscr{A}|
to interpret terms w.r.t. a valuation
\varsigma
.

Definition 4
(Interpretation)

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a commutative
\Sigma
-algebra. Suppose that
t\in\mathbb{F}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})
and consider a valuation
\varsigma
in
\mathfrak{A}
. The
interpretation

\llbracket t\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{A}}_{\varsigma}
is defined inductively as:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\hspace{1cm}}r}\begin{array}[]{rcl}\llbracket a\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{A}}_{\varsigma}&=&a^{\mathfrak{A}}\\
\llbracket[a]t\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{A}}_{\varsigma}&=&\mathtt{abs}^{\mathfrak{A}}(a,\llbracket t\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{A}}_{\varsigma})\\
\llbracket{\tt f^{\mathtt{C}}}(t_{1},t_{2})\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{A}}_{\varsigma}&=&f^{\mathtt{C},\mathfrak{A}}(\llbracket t_{1}\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{A}}_{\varsigma},\llbracket t_{2}\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{A}}_{\varsigma})\\
\end{array}\hfil\hskip 28.45274pt&\begin{array}[]{rcl}\llbracket\pi\cdot X\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{A}}_{\varsigma}&=&\pi\cdot\varsigma(X)\\
\llbracket{\tt f}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{A}}_{\varsigma}&=&f^{\mathfrak{A}}(\llbracket t_{1}\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{A}}_{\varsigma},\ldots,\llbracket t_{n}\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{A}}_{\varsigma})\\
\end{array}\end{array}

The interpretation map
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{A}}_{\varsigma}
is equivariant. The next definition gives a semantics to our derivations within nominal sets.
For any commutative
\Sigma
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
:

•

\llbracket\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{A}}_{\varsigma}
is
valid
if, and only if,
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.\pi\cdot\llbracket X\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{A}}_{\varsigma}=\llbracket X\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{A}}_{\varsigma}
, for each
\pi\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\in\Upsilon
;

•

\llbracket\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash t\approx_{\tt C}u\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{A}}_{\varsigma}
is
valid
if, and only if,
\llbracket\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{A}}_{\varsigma}
(valid) implies
\llbracket t\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{A}}_{\varsigma}=\llbracket u\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{A}}_{\varsigma}
.

More generally,
\llbracket\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash t\approx_{\tt C}u\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{A}}
is
valid
iff
\llbracket\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash t\approx_{\tt C}u\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{A}}_{\varsigma}
is valid for all valuation
\varsigma
.
The semantics of equality constraints is defined for all commutative
\Sigma
-algebras:
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vDash s\approx_{\tt C}t
when
\llbracket\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash s\approx_{\tt C}t\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{A}}
is valid for all
\mathfrak{A}
model of
\mathtt{C}
.

Example 7

An example of an algebra involves the set
\mathcal{P}_{\mathtt{fin}}(\mathbb{A})
. When equipped with the maps
\mathtt{Atm}(a)=\{a\},\mathtt{abs}(S)=S\setminus\{a\}
, and
f(S_{1},\ldots,S_{n})=\bigcap_{i}S_{i}
for every
\mathtt{f}:n
in
\Sigma
, it forms a model of
\mathtt{C}
.

The proof system is sound and complete with respect to the nominal set semantics.
In previous work, the
({{\approx}}_{\tt C}\,\mathbf{var})
rule was simpler: it checked only the domains of the permutations involved, yielding a restricted notion of soundness. The construction of the subgroup
\mathtt{P}_{\scalebox{0.5}{$$}}(\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}|_{X})
in
({{\approx}}_{\tt C}\,\mathbf{var})
is essential to obtain:

Theorem 4.2

For any
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}},t,u
, the following hold:

1.

(Soundness) If
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash t\approx_{\tt C}u
then
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vDash t\approx_{\tt C}u
.

2.

(Completeness) If
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vDash t\approx_{\tt C}u
then
\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\vdash t\approx_{\tt C}u
.

5
Nominal
\mathtt{C}
-Unification

In this section,
we present a rule-based algorithm for solving
\mathtt{C}
-unification problems using fixed-point constraints, proving its termination and correctness.

Definition 5
(Nominal
\mathtt{C}
-Unification Problem)

A
(nominal)
\mathtt{C}
-unification problem

\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}
has the form
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.Pr
, where
Pr
consists of a finite set of (unification) constraints of the form
s\approx_{\tt C}^{?}t
.

Solutions for nominal
\mathtt{C}
-unification problems will be expressed using pairs
\left\langle\Psi,\sigma\right\rangle_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}^{\prime}}}
consisting of a generalised fixed-point context
\Psi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}^{\prime}}}
and a substitution
\sigma
, where occurrences of the atoms
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}^{\prime}}
in the image of
\sigma
are

\mathsf{N}

-quantified.
We call such
\sigma
a

\mathsf{N}

-substitution, denoted as
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.\sigma
or
\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
. We will omit the subscript and write only
\sigma
to avoid clutter.
We will come back to this later in §
5.1
.

Definition 6
(
\mathtt{C}
-Solution/Unifier)

A
\mathtt{C}
-unifier (
\mathtt{C}
-
solution
) for a nominal
\mathtt{C}
-unification problem
\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}=\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.Pr
is a pair
\left\langle\Psi,\sigma\right\rangle_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}^{\prime}}}
, such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}^{\prime}}
and

\Psi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}^{\prime}}}\vdash s\sigma\approx_{\tt C}t\sigma
, for each
s\approx_{\tt C}^{?}t\in Pr
.
If no
\mathtt{C}
-unifier
\left\langle\Psi,\sigma\right\rangle_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}^{\prime}}}
exists for
\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}
, the problem
\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}
is
unsolvable
. We denote the set of all
\mathtt{C}
-unifiers of
\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}
by
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{P}_{\tt C})
.

For simplicity, we will omit
\mathtt{C}
and primarily refer to
\mathtt{C}
-unifiers and
\mathtt{C}
-solutions of
\mathtt{C}
-unification problems as unifiers and solutions, respectively.
The solutions to a problem
\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}
, elements of the set
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{P}_{\tt C})
, can be compared using the instantiation order
\lesssim
(quasi-order) defined as follows:

Definition 7
(Instantiation order)

Given
\left\langle\Psi_{1},\sigma_{1}\right\rangle_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}_{1}}}
and
\left\langle\Psi_{2},\sigma_{2}\right\rangle_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}_{2}}}
,
we write
\left\langle\Psi_{1},\sigma_{1}\right\rangle_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}_{1}}}\lesssim\left\langle\Psi_{2},\sigma_{2}\right\rangle_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}_{2}}}
when
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}_{1}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}_{2}}
and there exist a substitution
\delta
such that
(\Psi_{2})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}_{2}}}\vdash\Psi_{1}\delta
and
(\Psi_{2})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}_{2}}}\vdash X\sigma_{2}\approx_{\tt C}X\sigma_{1}\delta
, for all
X\in\mathbb{V}
.

For example, for
\sigma=[Y\mapsto a,X\mapsto Z]
,
\sigma^{\prime}=[Y\mapsto a,X\mapsto\mathtt{f}(Y,d),Z\mapsto\mathtt{f}(Y,d)]
, we can verify that

\left\langle\{(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c})\curlywedge_{\tt C}Z\},\sigma\right\rangle_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c}}\lesssim\left\langle\{(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c})\curlywedge_{\tt C}Z,(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})\curlywedge_{\tt C}Y\},\sigma^{\prime}\right\rangle_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}}
.
In fact, for
\delta=[Z\mapsto\mathtt{f}(Y,d)]
:

1.

\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}.\{(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c})\curlywedge_{\tt C}Z,(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})\curlywedge_{\tt C}Y\}\vdash W\sigma^{\prime}\approx_{\tt c}W\sigma\delta
, for all
W\in\mathbb{V}
.

2.

\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}.\{(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c})\curlywedge_{\tt C}Z,(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})\curlywedge_{\tt C}Y\}\vdash(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c})\curlywedge_{\tt C}Z\delta
.

Unlike nominal unification
[
24
]
(without equational theories), solvable nominal
\mathtt{C}
-unification problems need not have just one most general unifier. Thus, the role of the most general unifier is taken on by a complete set of
\mathtt{C}
-unifiers:

Definition 8
(Complete Set of
\mathtt{C}
-unifiers)

Let
\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}
be a
\mathtt{C}
-unification problem. A
complete set of
\mathtt{C}
-unifiers of
\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}
is a set of pairs
S
that satisfies

1.

S\subseteq\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{P}_{\tt C})
;

2.

For all
\left\langle\Psi,\sigma\right\rangle_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}^{\prime}}}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{P}_{\tt C})
there exists
\left\langle\Phi,\sigma^{\prime}\right\rangle_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}^{\prime\prime}}}\in S
s.t.
\left\langle\Phi,\sigma^{\prime}\right\rangle_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}^{\prime\prime}}}\lesssim\left\langle\Psi,\sigma\right\rangle_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}^{\prime}}}
.

5.1
\texttt{Unify}_{\mathtt{C}}
: a rule-based algorithm for
\mathtt{C}
-unification

(del)

\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.Pr\uplus\{t\approx_{\tt C}^{?}t\}

\Longrightarrow

\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.Pr

(\mathtt{f})

\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.Pr\uplus\{\mathtt{f}(\tilde{t})_{n}\approx_{\tt C}^{?}\mathtt{f}(\tilde{t^{\prime}})_{n}\}

\Longrightarrow

\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.Pr\cup\{t_{1}\approx_{\tt C}^{?}t_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,t_{n}\approx_{\tt C}^{?}t_{n}^{\prime}\}

(\mathtt{f^{C}})

\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.Pr\uplus\{\mathtt{f^{C}}(t_{0},t_{1})\approx_{\tt C}^{?}\mathtt{f^{C}}(s_{0},s_{1})\}

\Longrightarrow

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.Pr\cup\{t_{0}\approx_{\tt C}^{?}s_{0},t_{1}\approx_{\tt C}^{?}s_{1}\},\\[1.42271pt]
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.Pr\cup\{t_{0}\approx_{\tt C}^{?}s_{1},t_{1}\approx_{\tt C}^{?}s_{0}\}\end{array}\right.

([a])

\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.Pr\uplus\{[a]t\approx_{\tt C}^{?}[a]t^{\prime}\}

\Longrightarrow

\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.Pr\cup\{t\approx_{\tt C}^{?}t^{\prime}\}

(ab)

\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.Pr\uplus\{[a]t\approx_{\tt C}^{?}[b]t^{\prime}\}

\Longrightarrow

\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}.Pr\cup\{(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})\cdot t\approx_{\tt C}^{?}(b\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})\cdot t^{\prime}\}

(var)

\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.Pr\uplus\{\pi\cdot X\approx_{\tt C}^{?}\pi^{\prime}\cdot X\}

\Longrightarrow

\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.Pr\cup\{(\pi^{\prime-1}\circ\pi)\cdot X\approx_{\tt C}^{?}X\}
,

if
\pi^{\prime}\neq\mathtt{id}

(inst_{1})

\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.Pr\uplus\{\pi\cdot X\approx_{\tt C}^{?}t\}

\overset{[X\mapsto\pi^{-1}\cdot t]}{\Longrightarrow}

\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.Pr\{X\mapsto\pi^{-1}\cdot t\}
, if
X\notin\mathtt{Var}(t)

(inst_{2})

\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.Pr\uplus\{t\approx_{\tt C}^{?}\pi\cdot X\}

\overset{[X\mapsto\pi^{-1}\cdot t]}{\Longrightarrow}

\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.Pr\{X\mapsto\pi^{-1}\cdot t\}
, if
X\notin\mathtt{Var}(t)

Figure 2:
Simplification rules for
\approx_{\tt C}
. The notation
(\tilde{t})_{n}
abbreviates
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
.

In this subsection, we introduce a set of
simplification rules
(Figure
2
) for “solving” nominal
\mathtt{C}
-unification problems. These rules simplify problems by transforming constraints into “simpler” ones, by running the derivation rules from Figure
1
in reverse. The rule
({{\approx}}_{\tt C}\,\mathbf{\mathtt{f^{C}}})
splits a
\mathtt{C}
-unification problem into two, with the original problem solvable if at least one of the new problems is solvable. This requires working with finite sets of
\mathtt{C}
-unification problems, called
extended
\mathtt{C}
-unification problems
, denoted as
\mathcal{M}
.

The algorithm
{\tt Unif}_{\mathtt{C}}
starts with
{\cal M}=\{\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}\}
and exhaustively applies simplification rules.
We denote by
\left\langle{\cal M}\right\rangle_{\mathtt{nf}}
the normal forms of
{\cal M}
by the reduction relation
\Longrightarrow
generated by the simplification rules.

Theorem 5.1
(Termination)

The relation
\Longrightarrow
is terminating.

Some constraints in
\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}
cannot be simplified, we say that they are
reduced
. An equality constraint
s\approx_{\tt C}^{?}t
is
reduced
if it satisfies one of the following:

•

s
is a suspension on a variable and
t
is that variable (e.g.,
\pi\cdot X\approx_{\tt C}^{?}X
).

•

s
and
t
are distinct atoms (e.g.
a\approx_{\tt C}^{?}b
).

•

s
and
t
have different head symbols (e.g.,
\mathtt{f}(u)\approx_{\tt C}^{?}\mathtt{g}(v)
).

•

s
and
t
have different root constructors (e.g.,
[a]s^{\prime}\approx_{\tt C}^{?}\mathtt{f}(t)
).

•

s
is a suspension on some variable,
t
is not a suspension, but
t
contains occurrences of that variable (e.g.
X\approx_{\tt C}^{?}\mathtt{f}(X,a)
).

The first form, which is a fixed-point equation, is
consistent
; the others are
inconsistent
. A problem
\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}=\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.Pr
is
reduced
if all constraints in
Pr
are reduced.
A problem
\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}
is called
solvable
if its normal form
\left\langle\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}\right\rangle_{\mathtt{nf}}
contains a reduced problem where all the constraints are consistent.
In this case, we compute
\left\langle\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}\right\rangle_{\mathtt{sol}}
as the set of pairs
\left\langle\Upsilon,\sigma\right\rangle_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}^{\prime}}}

where

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}^{\prime}}
includes the set of names generated in the simplification process (i.e.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}^{\prime}}
),

\Upsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}^{\prime}}}=\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}^{\prime}}.\{\pi\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\mid\pi\cdot X\approx_{\tt C}^{?}X\in{\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}^{\prime}}\text{~solvable~and~}{\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}^{\prime}}\in\left\langle\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}\right\rangle_{\mathtt{nf}}\}
, and
\sigma
is either
Id
(if no instantiation rules were used) or the composition of substitutions used throughout the simplification of a branch starting in
\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}
and ending in
\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}^{\prime}
.

Example 8

Suppose
\Sigma=\{\wedge:2\}
. Let
\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}=\{[a](X\wedge Y)\approx_{\tt C}^{?}[b](Y\wedge X)\}
. Then

\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}\overset{*}{\Longrightarrow}\{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}.\{(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}\ b)\cdot X\approx_{\tt C}^{?}X\},\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}.\{(a\ b\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})\cdot X\approx_{\tt C}^{?}X,(a\ b\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})\cdot Y\approx_{\tt C}^{?}Y\}\}.

The algorithm computes two solutions:

\left\langle\{(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}\ b)\curlywedge_{\tt C}X\},[Y\mapsto(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}\ b)\cdot X]\right\rangle_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}}
and

\left\langle\{(a\ b\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})\curlywedge_{\tt C}X,(a\ b\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}})\curlywedge_{\tt C}Y\},Id\right\rangle_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}}
(a complete set of
\mathtt{C}
-solutions of
\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}
). Note that, by taking
\delta=[Y\mapsto X]
, the first is an instance of the second.

Remark 1

Substitutions feature

\mathsf{N}

-quantified names, as in
\sigma=[Y\mapsto(a\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}\ b)\cdot X]
. This is a desirable property that shows that solutions are stable under the choice of new names for renaming that can be done throughout computation. This is achieved using the concept of a

\mathsf{N}

-substitution,
which provides a systematic way to track freshness information.

The next result shows that if a
\mathtt{C}
-unification problem
\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}
is solvable, our algorithm
{\tt Unif}_{\mathtt{C}}
will output
\left\langle\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}\right\rangle_{\mathtt{sol}}
which is a complete set of solutions of
\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}
.

Theorem 5.2
(Correctness)

Let
\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}
be a problem such that
\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}\overset{*}{\Longrightarrow}\left\langle\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}\right\rangle_{\mathtt{nf}}
. If
\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}
is solvable, then the following hold:

1.

(Soundness)

\left\langle\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}\right\rangle_{\mathtt{sol}}\subseteq\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{P}_{\tt C})
.

2.

(Completeness)
If
\left\langle\Phi,\tau\right\rangle_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}^{\prime\prime}}}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{P}_{\tt C})
then there exists
\left\langle\Psi,\sigma\right\rangle_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}^{\prime}}}\in\left\langle\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}\right\rangle_{\mathtt{sol}}
s.t.
\left\langle\Psi,\sigma\right\rangle_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}^{\prime}}}\lesssim\left\langle\Phi,\tau\right\rangle_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}^{\prime\prime}}}
. The set
\left\langle\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}\right\rangle_{\mathtt{sol}}
is a complete set of
\mathtt{C}
-unifiers of
\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}
.

A direct consequence of termination and correctness is that the complete set of
\mathtt{C}
-unifiers of a
\mathcal{P}_{\tt C}
is always finite.

Corollary 1

\mathtt{C}
-unification using generalised fixed-point constraints is finitary.

6
Related Work.

Gabbay and Mathijssen
[
18
]
define a sound and complete proof system for nominal algebra, where derivation rules are subject to freshness conditions,
and provide a semantics in the class of nominal sets. Nominal sets also give an absolute denotation for first-order logic
[
17
]
, and more recently for predicate logic
[
13
]
.

Applications of nominal techniques in languages where operators satisfy equational axioms were studied in various contexts, using freshness constraints in the definition of
\alpha
-equivalence: e.g., to specify syntactic reasoning algorithms to show properties of program transformations on higher-order expressions in call-by-need functional languages, taking into account e.g.
letrec
constructs
[
22
]
; in theorem-proving, due to nominal logic’s equivariance property, a different form of unification, called equivariant unification, was investigated in
[
11
]
;
applications in theorem-proving and rewrite-based programming languages also require the use e.g. of
A
and/or
C
operators
[
6
]
. Nominal unification algorithms modulo commutativity are available, which generate infinite complete sets of solutions expressed using freshness constraints and substitutions
[
3
,
4
]
.
The finitary algorithm developed here opens the path for extensions of programming languages such as
\alpha
-prolog
[
12
]
, building commutativity into the unification algorithm.

Alternative approaches to representing binders, beyond the already-mentioned HOAS, include the
\lambda\sigma
-calculus
[
1
]
and the locally nameless representation
[
10
]
. The
\lambda\sigma
-calculus is based on the definition of explicit capture-avoiding substitutions. However, a significant drawback is that it does not support reasoning about open terms, as it lacks confluence when applied to terms with meta-variables.
Handling meta-variables effectively is essential for reasoning about binders in in proof assistants, such as AutoSubst2
[
23
]
, which provides support for formalising metatheory in Coq. It is based on the
\lambda\sigma
-calculus and could benefit from an alternative approach to computing substitutions for meta-variables—one that systematically accounts for the required renamings.

7
Conclusion

We introduced a novel framework for equational reasoning modulo commutativity in languages with binders, using nominal techniques with (

\mathsf{N}

-quantified) permutation fixed-point constraints. This approach integrates commutativity into
\alpha
-equivalence reasoning without relying on freshness constraints, ensuring soundness and completeness in nominal set semantics.
Additionally, we proposed a terminating and correct
\mathtt{C}
-unification algorithm that provides finite complete set of solutions, addressing a key limitation of previous methods that produced infinite solutions when using freshness constraints. Our results establish a solid foundation for reasoning about syntax with binding and equational properties, with potential applications in theorem proving, programming language semantics, and automated reasoning systems.

Future work includes extending our approach to broader equational theories, e.g. integrating associativity along commutativity, and exploring practical implementations in verification tools.

Appendix 0.A
Proofs of Section
3

See
1

Proof

1.

If
\pi
is a product of disjoint cycles, then write
\pi=\mu_{1}\circ\ldots\circ\mu_{n}
. Note that
\eta
is one of these
\mu_{i}
’s, say
\eta=\mu_{i_{0}}
. Then
a\in\mathtt{dom}(\mu_{i_{0}})-\mathtt{supp}(x)
. Suppose, by contradiction, that there is an
b\in\mathtt{dom}(\mu_{i_{0}})\cap\mathtt{supp}(x)
. Then
b\in\mathtt{supp}(x)
implies
\mu_{n}(b)\in\mathtt{supp}(\mu_{n}\cdot x)
and since
\mu_{n}(b)=b
this leads to
b\in\mathtt{supp}(\mu_{n}\cdot x)
, which in turn yields
\mu_{n-1}(b)\in\mathtt{supp}((\mu_{n-1}\mu_{n})\cdot x)
, that is equivalent to
b\in\mathtt{supp}((\mu_{n-1}\mu_{n})\cdot x)
because
\mu_{n-1}(b)=b
. Proceeding this way, eventually we will reach
b\in\mathtt{supp}((\mu_{i_{0}+1}\ldots\mu_{n-1}\mu_{n})\cdot x)
. Applying
\mu_{i}
on both sides yields

\mu_{i_{0}}(b)\in\mathtt{supp}((\mu_{i_{0}}\mu_{i_{0}+1}\ldots\mu_{n-1}\mu_{n})\cdot x).

Since
\mu_{i_{0}-1}
and
\mu_{i_{0}}
are disjoint, we have
\mu_{i_{0}-1}(\mu_{i_{0}}(b))=\mu_{i_{0}}(b)
and so we get

\mu_{i_{0}}(b)\in\mathtt{supp}((\mu_{i_{0}-1}\mu_{i_{0}}\mu_{i_{0}+1}\ldots\mu_{n})\cdot x).

Therefore, applying the rest of the cycles, we obtain
\mu_{i_{0}}(b)\in\mathtt{supp}(\pi\cdot x)
and, consequently,
\mu_{i_{0}}(b)\in\mathtt{supp}(x)
because
\pi\cdot x=x
. Since there is a
l>0
such that
\mu_{i_{0}}^{l}(b)=a
, by repeating this argument as many times as necessary, we obtain
a=\mu_{i_{0}}^{l}(b)\in\mathtt{supp}(x)
, reaching a contradiction.

2.

First, note that
\eta_{i}\cdot x=x
iff
\eta_{i}^{-1}\cdot x=x
. Since
\rho\in\left\langle\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n}\right\rangle
, there is a
r\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\rho=\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}\ldots\zeta_{r}
where
\zeta_{i}\in\{\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n}\}
or
\zeta_{i}\in\{\eta_{1}^{-1},\ldots,\eta_{n}^{-1}\}
. Thus,
\zeta_{i}\cdot x=x
for all
i=1,\ldots,r
. Therefore,

(\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}\ldots\zeta_{r})\cdot x=(\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}\ldots\zeta_{r-1})\cdot(\zeta_{r}\cdot x)=(\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}\ldots\zeta_{r-1})\cdot x=\ldots=\zeta_{1}\cdot x=x.

See
2

Proof

1.

Suppose
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.\pi\cdot x=x
. By definition,
D_{1}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}\mid\pi\cdot x=x\}
is cofinite. Since the set
D_{2}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}\cap\mathtt{supp}(x)=\emptyset\}
is cofinite, it follows that
D_{1}\cap D_{2}
is still cofinite. Take
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}\in D_{1}\cap D_{2}
. Then
\pi\cdot x=x
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}\cap\mathtt{supp}(x)=\emptyset
hold simultaneously. Since
\pi\cdot x=x
and each cycle of
\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
mention atoms from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}
, it follows by Lemma
1
that
\mathtt{dom}(\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}})\cap\mathtt{supp}(x)=\emptyset
and, by the definition of support,
\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\cdot x=x
.

Now, it remains to prove that
\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\cdot x=x
. Using the fact that
\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
and
\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
commute because they are disjoint, we get

\displaystyle\pi\cdot x=x

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow(\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\circ\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}})\cdot x=x

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow(\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\circ\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}})\cdot x=x

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\cdot(\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\cdot x)=x

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\cdot x=x.

Thus, the sets
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}\mid\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\cdot x=x\}
and
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}\mid\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\cdot x=x\}
are cofinite and the result follows.

Conversely, suppose
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\cdot x=x
and
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\cdot x=x
. This implies that the sets
D_{1}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}\mid\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\cdot x=x\}
and
D_{2}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}\mid\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\cdot x=x\}
are cofinite. By assumption, both
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
are cofinite. Consequently, their intersection
D_{1}\cap D_{2}
is also cofinite. For all
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}\in D_{1}\cap D_{2}
,
\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\cdot x=x
and
\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\cdot x=x
hold simultaneously. Applying
\pi_{\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}
to both sides of
\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}\cdot x=x
, we obtain
\pi\cdot x=x
. Therefore,
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}\mid\pi\cdot x=x\}
is cofinite and so
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{N}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}.\pi\cdot x=x
.

2.

Suppose, by contradiction, that there is a
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}
satisfying
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}\cap\mathtt{supp}(x)=\emptyset
such that
\mathtt{dom}(\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}})\cap\mathtt{supp}(x)\neq\emptyset
. Then there is an atom
a
(that cannot be in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}
) such that
a\in\mathtt{dom}(\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}})
and
a\in\mathtt{supp}(x)
. Thus, for some
l\geq 1
, we can write

\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}=(a\ \pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}(a)\ \ldots\ \pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{c}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{c}}}^{l}(a))\circ\rho
